ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-11.ana.ud1232e170	Today, we are dealing with a position of great importance for the development of this country over a decade — and of course beyond that. So it seems that the committee has marked the enormous commitment of the whole country to this matter, both during the process of the proposal for the notification and during the work on the Committee's position. The commitment is natural, because good transport and communication opportunities are not just an objective in themselves, but a decisive tool to achieve almost all other policy objectives, which increase the value and competitiveness of business, to use the whole country, good welfare services in health, school, kindergarten and care for the elderly — and much more. We have developed a welfare society, a business and an international interaction that requires increased mobility. The transport sector has not followed this development. The sector has been down-prioritized by several governments over time. So we have a follow-up on maintenance. For this reason, the train is too often – in fact also today – and therefore the outcome ratio is too bad. A transport lift, a bet, is needed in this sector. I am therefore pleased that the Government has invited the Storting to adopt a very ambitious national transport plan for the next ten years, a proposal that was very well received throughout the country when it was submitted. The government wants to spend 100 billion more on communications over the next ten years compared to the current plan. That's an increase of 45 pst. In addition to this, county councils are given a significant increase to the road network, which they will take over from the new year. For 2010, this increase is DKK 1 billion, and one of the reasons is that the borders of the county roads will increase in the years to come. The NTP proposal includes comprehensive road, rail, ports and fairways development plans and a much stronger commitment to road safety, public transport and measures to facilitate pedestrians and sick people. There are also a number of measures to achieve our objectives of better climate and environment. It is a comprehensive approach that the Committee has put forward, in fact, on 300 pages. In a few areas, there is a consensual committee behind the comments; in other areas, there are different batch constellations. The game has shown both a policy and a financial framework in the configuration. And I'm sure that's what the different parties will do, so here I want to spend most of my time on the multi-party setting. I have particularly noted the progress of non-inferiority of money. In this setting there is an addition of a frame of 580 billion kroner, but according to the Official Journal of the Drama 5 June, the Ulf Erik Knudsen of the Progress Party says that they will spend 1 000 billion kroner, or a billion dollars, on road construction. One would think that the Progress Party had money other than what the rest of us do. It is easy to be a politician when you can promise billions of new ones for almost all good purposes and at the same time promise huge tax havens. The party of progress here owes some of us to lack political courage in the debate on transport. But how much political courage does it take to bet on billions of promises versus pursuing a responsible economic policy? How shall the Progress Party comply with its promises? They want to form a government with the Right – like the Christian People's Party and the Left. Although some of these parties in opposition also overwhelm the Government, they operate with completely different financial frameworks. A transport plan must be followed up in the annual government budget, but it is now the first time in the four-year period. The treatment of NTP for 2006–2015 resulted in more than one ambitious transport plan than proposed by the Bondevik government. After four years, the government has not only met the financial limits, with overfilling them by DKK 1.9 billion. The action package for work involves an additional purchase effort of around 3.8 billion kroner. This shows that we have managed to reverse a long-standing negative trend and that we have started a necessary transport lift. National transport plans are structured around the key objectives of the future and regional development, transport security, environment and universal design. In particular, we would stress that the NTP contains a clearly differentiated transport policy with a particular focus on public transport and rail in and around the largest cities, while the growth of the economic framework is particularly strong in the country. The majority of major investments in rail are in the intercity triangle in the Austland area. The double-track Oslo-Ski is the second largest single project in the NTP. There is a large amount of road project in the investment plan – 35 on E6, which extends from Svinesund to Kirkenes. Nearer 40 road projects shall start already during the first four-year period. There are projects in ports and fairways along the whole coast, securing fairways, deepening ports and expanding new port facilities for landing fish. In particular, North Norway is satisfied with many port projects. The NTP Nordgebiet bet also involves several important development projects in relation to the roads to Russia, Finland and Sweden. In the context of the Northern Region initiative, it is very good to now create a holistic study that looks at the long-term transport needs and the different expansion options in the north, including the possibility of rail. When it comes to the railways or the country, I feel that there is a much greater cross-political will to bet now than it was a few years ago. The government is investing heavily in the years ahead, including: with double track expansion in the intercity triangle, with the development of terminals and crossing tracks. There will be a doubling of the freight capacity. In addition, the potential of a high-speed path in Norway should be assessed. With the exception of the Progress Party, all parties say that this arrangement should aim at the construction of a high-speed path. But we don't have a good enough basis for decision now, and we also say that the statement will show if it's possible to achieve this goal. Different professional environments make different assessments, and we need a more robust basis to make a decision when the transport plan rolls in four years. This arrangement must be taken into account by the State, but there must be a shift in the work done by the external railway professional environment. And then I want to stress that we're going to build up existing paths in line with NTP's plans. We also say that the intercity wager in the NTP proposal can be a first step in a future high-speed path. The Government Party is concerned that the development project will be carried out efficiently, collectively and with as rapid progress as possible. This adds up to the NTP proposal, because there are significantly increased frames and several large, coherent development projects. We are in favour of the Government's proposal to try out a mechanism to segregate some large, whole-scale investment projects in their own budget lines in each budget year. The project with a plan of progress and funding must be dealt with by Stortinget, and the rewards for years to come must be followed up in order to achieve maximum rational progress. We also see that new forms of contract that combine construction/development, operation and maintenance of road sections can contribute to good use of resources. The evaluation of the OPS project shows that this is a positive experience that we can take with us. Better road safety is an overarching goal. It's tragic that so many people are killed and injured in traffic. The zero vision must be constant. It is good that the NTP proposal has an ambitious goal of getting closer to this vision during the period. There is not one action here, but a series of actions that will work together. Several of these measures are particularly targeted at the high-risk group — those aged 15 to 24. I would like, among other things: indicate that a proposal to increase the financial envelope for the road safety programme area by 159 pst. compared to the existing NTP. In NTP it is proposed to build 500 km with new walking and cycling roads along the national road network. Let's add up the permissions by 154 pst. compare with the current plan. We are overconfident that better organisation for cyclists brings great social benefits in terms of better traffic management, less energy use, less air pollution, positive health benefits and less noise. Two thirds of all trips shorter than 5 km are carried out by car, so this is undoubtedly a great potential for more bicycle use. We think there needs to be offensive national effort for better and more bike paths. Transport accounts for nearly one third of CO2 emissions in Norway. In the national transport plan, the ambitious objectives of the climate communication are maintained. The target should be one mainly through reduced vehicle fleet emissions and reduced car traffic in metropolitan areas. Emissions per year Kilometers are already falling after the government's green conversion of the charges. Increased use of biofuels and active contributions to the electrification of the fleet will lead to emissions per year. kilometers further downwards. Reduced car traffic in big cities is a demanding but equally important task – a task that must be resolved in close and committed cooperation between the state, regions and municipalities. A key contribution from the State is to enable the railway to take on growth in traffic. The reward scheme is another government tool. The reward scheme and the project «Citys of the Future» are now to be joined and become one of the obligatory cooperation projects with reduced emissions targets. From 2008 to 2009 we double the reward scheme for public transport. Now the reward scheme is to be doubled again. It is part of a national transport plan. The reward scheme will then be 650 million. kr. And the increase will largely fall on the cities that want to agree with the state on a package of positive and restrictive measures. Kristiansand has recently concluded such an agreement, and in the environmental package for Trondheim, which Stortinget has to deal with, such an agreement is also signalled. There is a willingness to apply effective restrictive measures in many cities, and then the clear signal of the majority of the population is that we will contribute financially to financing, for example. increase public transport. The majority also give a clear signal that more stringent requirements should be imposed on urban packages in the future. Examples of requirements are land planning that reduces transport needs, demands for better collective provision and the development of a coherent bicycle road network. The aim is to reduce emissions by increasing public transport and bicycle use in cities. In the setting, the majority of the government is asking to set ambitious targets for public transport in conjunction with counties and city municipalities, for example. within ‘Citys of the future’. The setting also underlines the important role of express buses in long-distance transport between cities, and states and states must actively contribute to the further development of these good services in order to increase the number of passengers. The national transport plan contains many concrete measures to bring the transport sector closer to a universal design over the next ten years, and the disabled organisations – at least many of them – have, in their hearings, been pleased that they are going to work concretely and obligingly for everyone to join, including on buses and trains. Finally, the government has put forward an NTP proposal that is a powerful transport lift, a lift that we need. The Stoltenberg government has shown during this period that they have the will and ability to implement an attached transport plan. The same government will also do with the proposed plan.

In Norway, we are struggling with roads over 100 years of age. In 1962 a motorway plan was adopted in this house, and only a few miles have been completed. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-11.ana.ud1232e818	The alternative national transport plan of the left is entitled ‘Green and Modern Norway’. The government’s proposal for a national transport plan contains many good projects, but the absolute greatest weakness of the government’s NTP proposal is, as several organisations also pointed out during the hearings, the lack of collective action in our cities. It is not possible to build itself out of queue and congestion in already heavily pressed urban areas. We expect strong population growth in the years to come. The funds allocated shall also be made available for the planning and development of urban railways, such as the urban railways in Bergen and on the North Jären, Kolsåsbanen, the new Fornebubane and the path to Akershu University Hospital. These are important projects that will provide significantly better and more forward-looking collective services in the cities concerned. Within the railway area, the Left proposes a package of projects which together will result in significantly reduced journey time on the railway. The aim is to reduce the travel time by 2020 in the intercity triangle Oslo-Skien, Oslo-Halden, Oslo-Lillehammer by about 80 minutes, a trip time between Oslo and Kristiansand in three hours, and a travel time between Oslo-Bergen and Oslo-Trondheim in four hours. Prior to the completion of the Committee's proceedings, the government parties sold out that there was a majority for high-speed trains in Norway and that the government parties on the Stortinget had gone further than the Government. It's just on paper. The government has not added one additional crown to such a bet. This is to put the selectors behind the light. With this, government parties with SV at the head push the bet on a more modern railway in front of them, by trying to sell out new investigations and talks, instead of concrete actions. I want to take the strongest distance from this type of populism from the extreme left wing. Sv settles here with cheap headlines in the media rather than fighting for the issues the party itself has stated that they will bet on, and that matter in real life. A shining example of the airlock high-speed train is that they do not even propose to realize the new Ringeriks track between Sandvika and Henefoss during the period. This path is part of most high-speed concept studies in Norway and would be the natural starting point for starting a high-speed initiative. It is alarming that, after four years in majority management, SV has not been fully financed by the Ringerik tunnel in their plans. The left supports the comments in the setting that deal with high-speed trains, but the difference between the government parties and the left is that at the same time we follow up with NOK 30 billion more to a modern railway. We have this, among other things. taken from the road budget. The left prioritizes the railway. In addition to investing heavily on a more modern infrastructure, the Left, together with the other opposition parties, proposes a number of modernisation measures. Modernisation gives more impetus to the resources made available by the community. It became more expensive for the money when Mesta was separated from the State Road Administration and there was a full competition for the tasks. It was a better offer for the travellers when the Gjøvik Railway was put on offer. The left will continue to compete with the train traffic and end the NSB train monopoly. This says the government's parties repeatedly refuse to do so. In this connection, I would like to refer to a quote from Minister of Transport Liv Signe Navarsete in the Class Battle on Thursday, March 5, 2009, where she said: ‘Tendering policy in the railway sector is firm. My impression is that this policy is effective in terms of efficiency and profitability.’ This assessment agrees with the Left. Recently, the Stortinget, by a broad majority, joined the Government's proposal for amendments to the Professional Transport Act and the Railway Act, which safeguard the rights of employees when using competition on the track. This provides a good basis for continuing work on competition. Since the Minister of Transport himself determines that the experience of competition is positive and the rights of employees are guaranteed, the Government and the Government’s continuing resistance to the use of competition in public transport must be a clear expression of an ideological approach and the close ties of the government parties to the trade unions. The left will also outsource all State entrepreneurship under the direction of the railway works. This does not want government parties. They want to go the other way, namely to consider restoring state entrepreneurship in the state road system. I would strongly warn against that. The catchment made by the establishment of Mesta AS has helped to restore the Community's resources, estimated at about DKK 1 billion per year. Left will also use OPS contracts for the construction of new major road and route sections. Experience from OPS projects shows up to half the construction time. This means that OPS contracts increase the societal benefits of the projects by allowing people and businesses to enjoy the infrastructure much earlier. OPS contracts also open to a greater extent to employ foreign contractors in order to increase competition for such jobs in Norway. Increased competition may lower costs. Through the increased investment on the road and by shifting more traffic from road to track and keel, the Left will provide a safer and better road network throughout the country. In addition to the human suffering, the society incurs 26 billion kroner in annual costs. Priority must be given to safe roads. The left will further prioritise the development of the Stavanger–Bergen–Ålesund–Trondheim coastal route and neighbouring road networks. The Western region accounts for a large part of the value creation in Norway. It is important to ensure close access to the market and to expand the labour market in that part of the country. In order to strengthen the systematic work on road safety, the Left will also establish independent roadside supervision, in line with that established in aviation and rail. As regards Oslo Package 3, the majority of the Committee wished to deal with this matter with NTP. This I believe is unwise, and I want to use a three-minute post later today on this toll package. I just want to point out here something that the representative Trond Helleland might also mention during his treatment of Oslopakt 3, namely a sentence where we criticize the travel agency in Oslo. However, there is no need to do so. As the Left, Right and Christian People's Party refers to in its comments on the same subject, Oslo and Akershus are one of the urban areas in Norway that implements the most offensive effort on public transport. About two thirds of all public transport in Norway takes place in Oslo and Akershus. In Oslo, car traffic has been reduced by a total of 8 pst. so far in 2009. The number of passengers in public transport has increased by 14 million in 2008. Similarly, there was a growth of over 6 pst. by public travellers in Akershus. An improved route offer and lower prices are key to success. Few other cities in Norway have the same results as in Oslo. It may seem that the political color of city leaders is more important than what is achieved, but I will come back to that later during the treatment of the environmental package in Trondheim. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-11.ana.ud1232e937	In just a few days, it has been five years since the Stortinget discussed the national transport plan for the period 2006–2015. The starting point today is a completely different one from that one. We now have a growing period where the investment in transport is strongly strengthened, and we have put forward a national transport plan that also means a big boost. The setting indicates that there is a broad consensus that there will now be a strong commitment to upgrading transport infrastructure throughout the country. There is also a great difference between the debate in this Chamber five years ago. I'm very pleased with that. This is a good promise for transportation in this country. Nevertheless, I hear some representatives repeating the claim that this government has so much more money to spend than the previous government. If you look at page 67 of the National Transport Plan, you will see that it is very clear that one has the same room for action in the undergrowth that the previous government, the Bondevik II government, had. I can't see that it has joined the configuration. But this government doesn't give priority to tax havens. This government prioritises the large amounts of money for the important issues, including social education. In previous National Transport Plan proposals, there has been a cautious increase in the framework — yes, even reduced frameworks. This time we notify an increase of 45 pst. We're going to continue to build the country. This calls for a strong commitment to the transport sector over a long period of time. The transport strategy over the next ten years has highlighted a good balance between the various objectives of the transport sector. Transport policy shall contribute to strengthening the competitiveness of industry and facilitate growth in business and welfare in the future. At the same time, we have an overarching objective of moving a good step forward towards the zero vision of road safety, and we will strengthen our efforts to turn the transport sector in a more environmentally friendly direction. At least through the organisation of the transport system, we shall contribute to a wider community that is inclusive and accessible to all. Since the current government parties together with the Progress Party raised the framework of the previous national transport plan, this was characterised as an airlock that would help to weaken the confidence of politicians in general and in national transport plans in particular. There was so much doubt about our ability and willingness to follow this up in the annual budgets. We have followed up, and with the broad political agreement on raising the transport sector, I have no doubt that the bold framework we are going for this time, and will be followed up. If we are to bet on transport, we must first and foremost allow more of the state budget. It seems that some people think that OPS is a magic form that provides free resources. It is not. OPS is purchase by hire, with the costs associated with it. Norway has a wealth of money that makes it artificial to make the issue of increasing transport investment into a NM in creative bookkeeping. There are some things that speak for OPS, but OPS cannot take political responsibility for prioritising us. There is no reason for the government to go on trying to buy ways to pay. We don't need it in a country with strong government finances like Norway. Furthermore, today we have room for action, not after 2020, when the burden of the OPS payment comes too fully. The positive elements are on the organisation page of the OPS project. We will continue this by developing new forms of contract, e.g.: where a contractor is responsible for remedial action, maintenance and operation of a longer distance over several years. Governments take into account the different challenges in different parts of the country. We need to use other tools in cities than in the districts. In District Norway, the road is often the only option, and then the bet has to be about the road. It is important to see roads, railways, ports and aviation in the context. While the rise in rail routes mainly benefits the central routes, the roadways increase most in the rest of the country. The strong commitment to shipping, ports and fairway measures will benefit the entire coast. Providing Avinor with good conditions enables the company to further develop a supply of aviation that binds the country together, which is decisive for the growth of business and population in a scattered country like Norway. Some people think that roading is the same as building motorways, and they want to start with a four-field highway out of Oslo. When I meet representatives of the business community who use the roads to transport goods around the country, the image looks a little more nuanced. Then an operationally safe path that gives good punctuality, and a yellow middle stripe that allows large runs to cross each other safely, is equally important. At the same time, we give priority to major projects that can remove bottlenecks as a result of typography and traffic volumes in central strokes. More holistic standards on the road network will provide a whole new everyday life for those who have their working days along the road, and reduced travel time on important routes will result in savings in time and money. In cities, we will invest in rail, public transport and road-parking that will make entry effective, and roads that will make transit more efficient, rather than generally increase road capacity, as a means of contributing to further growth in car traffic. All research and international experience shows that it is not enough just to bet on better organisation of public transport and walking and cycling roads if one is going to dampen car use in cities. We must also dare to take the politically difficult choices through effective and restrictive measures in relation to car traffic. Here there has been a positive development in this period. Key actors such as LO, NHO and most transport user organisations recognise the need to combine offensive collective action with restrictive measures. That's good! Investments in major new road and rail projects are important for businesses and people most. We will build many such facilities in the years to come. There is often a lot of attention about such a project, but it helps little if the rest of the web is confused. The government has thus reinforced the commitment to operation and maintenance compared to the existing national transport plan. In the national transport plan we have now submitted, we propose to further increase the effort for operation and maintenance – until the level of transport rates requested in their contribution to the national transport plan. At the same time, efforts are becoming less spectacular, but often very profitable, less investment measures almost double. In total, this is now becoming so large that we have renamed them from ‘minor investment measures’ to ‘programme areas’. These include the removal of bottlenecks, capacity-enhancing measures, the extension of width, measures that provide a more universal design of the facilities, and not least important road safety measures. These measures are not so prestigious, but in some cases we get a lot more in important areas, and they provide great socio-economic benefits. For me, it has also been very important that we have found room to spend NOK 1 billion every year on racial protection – almost a triple from today’s national transport plan, and as much as the National Racial Protection Group has asked. I am pleased that a broad majority in the Committee will agree on the preparation of a plan to respond to the question of whether there is a basis for the construction of high-speed rail in Norway. It is important to have a good professional basis in place so that the government can decide on this major issue at a later stage. Consequently, the Government does not propose to initiate the construction of separate motorways in Norway in this transport plan. The main focus of the government is on building a high-capacity rail network in the intercity triangle. The strong initiative in the intercity triangle will provide a good basis for the future development of a high-speed network in Norway. Therefore, concrete assessments shall be made as to whether the concepts for several of these projects can be adapted to the possible development of high-speed rail. As in all other countries, greenhouse gas emissions are a major challenge. There is a need for a systematic shift of the transport system to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. The government has already taken important steps – such as the conversion of the one-time fee and the phase-in of biofuels – to reverse the trend of increasing emissions from the sector. In addition to increasing the investment on railways and other collective actions, we also invest heavily on pedestrian and cyclist roads. And it is an important measure that this year's budget will now be doubled in the first four-year period. Cities that want to take a force will be given proper drag to bring down their car usage. However, the car will always be important in a country like ours. People will also be allowed to drive in the future, but cars will have to move on to non-emission fuels. The rapid phase-in of low or zero-emission vehicles is important. It shall be achieved through the use of the charging system, support schemes through Transnova and research tools. The size now also treats Oslopackak 3 step 2. In addition to road procurement, the state makes significant concessions, and the state contribution to investment on the future state road network will also be important. Oslopackakt 3 is beyond the largest toll pack in history, and there is a broad cross-political settlement about it. There has been a long and extensive process locally, but the government has ensured expressive speed in the further processing, where we have put in place a legislative change in order to use toll revenue for the operation of public transport, and the package was divided into two phases in order to speed up the collection process itself. There has been a good dialogue with local authorities, and I want to give tribute to Oslo and Akershus for having a chance to see more actions in conjunction, for establishing cooperation on Oslopacket 3 and for the establishment of Router AS. It is important to want to develop a holistic transport system where buses are coordinated with trains, etc. Oslopackak 3 is a big effort to develop the road and public network in the capital area over the next 20 years, and I'm glad that we're getting this in place. Today is a great day for Samferdsle-Noreg. I am pleased that the Storting has great ambitions on behalf of the traffic of the coming ten years.

That is why all the parties, from SV to Right, now stand behind Oslopackak 3. Thus, the progressing party is fully on the sideline when dealing with the package in Stortinget. That's regrettable, because this message had deserved as much support here on the Stortinget as it has in Oslo. There is also the Progress Party to support the package. Oslopackak 3 is a toll project that has large local political connections, and is therefore also robust in view of fluctuations in this 20-year period that it is intended to handle. In step 1, we set up the financing system for the package, and we were able to start the collection already in 2008. This includes, among other things: Oslo was able to start with a seven-and-a-half-minute route on the Grorudban, which has led to a dramatic increase in the number of travelers. Projects such as Ulven-Sinsen and Kolsåsbanen could also start earlier than if the package were adopted as a whole, and not in two steps. At the previous treatment of Oslopackak 3 all parties, except the Advance Party, were included in a note where we said: ‘The majority are pleased that, through the treatment of Oslopackak 3 in two phases, transfers to the operation of public transport are now being started much earlier than if the package had been dealt with collectively.’ And on the other hand, ‘The majority are also pleased to see that important investment projects such as Ulven-Sinsen and Kolsåsbanen are also being launched again.’ It is therefore a little surprising to see that several of the parties now go away from this and write comments that may give the impression that Ulven–Sinsen is no longer part of Oslopackakt 3, despite the fact that the project is described in the agreement of the parties in Oslo and Akershus. Oslopackak 3 step 2 is a message to Stortinget about the management system of the package and a description of the contents of the package. Because the package is based on broad local agreement, it is important that the further work on the package is done through broad local anchoring between the parties that were part of the Oslopacket 3 compromise. Although local representatives are given the opportunity to follow the package through the treatment of various projects in Akershus County or Oslo City Council, the majority here in Stortinget believe that the local parties in the steering group must actively anchor against the parties and representatives in their respective local bodies. Yesterday there was a press release from the management group for Oslopackakt 3, where they described which projects they thought it would be invested in the next few years. Based on the reactions that have come on the phone and in the press, it must be said that this hardly had a wide lashing. This means that the undersigned now believe that the ministry should review this model, and at least ensure that the opposition is better represented, and ensure that we get the broad anchoring that all the parties have always agreed that we should have in the package. In consultation here at Stortinget, it has just been pointed out by the parties that this compromise has been reached and that it requires a broad local anchoring. The reactions to yesterday's press release indicate that this has not been the case. Opinions from the City Council for the Environment and Transport of the Baltic Sea region in Oslo also indicate that the comments made by a cross-political majority in the Grand Duchy have not been taken seriously, which says that there should be a balance between road and public transport and that the reduction of climate emissions should govern the work of the group. We have to make sure that we have a system that ensures that the parties that are part of that compromise are kept under constant review in the processes that will run for the next 20 years. This is the only way we can be sure to have a package that will survive changing majority constellations both locally and here at Stortinget in the next 20 years. Oslo does not deserve that we politicians now turn to what should have been meant to solve Oslo's future transport challenges and climate challenges, and also ensure a reduction in climate emissions, which is strongly highlighted by the Right, the Christian People's Party, the Left, the Workers' Party, the Centre Party and the SV in the joint comments on the Communication. It is therefore a broad majority that determines that consideration for the reduction of climate emissions must be the guiding force for the further development of Oslopackak 3. I believe that we are now ensuring the commitment to public transport through what we are doing here on the Stortinget, when a cross-political majority decides that any budgetary impact on large investment projects should not be at the expense of the effort we have on the operation of public transport. When this package is to be rolled every four years, it is important that those who run the package at all times are faithful to the agreement and the leaders we have put here on the Stortinget, and do not try to pursue party politics at the expense of it. The reward scheme for public transport is part of the NTP, but it is not part of the Oslo package 3. Despite this, the money from the reward scheme can help us achieve the objectives of reducing climate emissions from the transport sector. Therefore, it is also disappointing that urban councils for the environment and transport in Oslo have done nothing more to get out of the reward scheme for public transport. It is disappointing that the Progress Party is more concerned with political games than with gatherings for Oslo and Akershus. They are applying to the Ministry of Transport to create a toll package for Oslo and Akershus, but when it comes to Stortinget, they do not stand behind it. We also see the same duality with regard to the reward scheme. The Progress Party Agency complains that Oslo does not receive enough money from the reward scheme, but refuses to engage in dialogue with the Ministry on what measures can be taken to take more of the money agreed by the majority here on Stortinget in the climate agreement, would be given to the urban areas that were willing to enter into binding, long-term agreements to reduce car traffic. There's the ball now, at the city council, and for Oslo's sake, I hope he'll take it further. It is therefore positive that it could be possible to raise capital by, for example: private property developers or others contribute capital into the road projects in exchange for, for example: can be built on top of the lids built or built on top of the existing road, if installed in a tunnel. This kind of solution could provide an opportunity to get started a long time ago, or that we're at all able to implement the projects we've been aiming to do. Areas such as the E18 westwards, the E6 in the Groruddalen or the Mossevejen from the centre to the Wurvenbukta – if we manage to realize these projects – could appear as clean natural and open-air areas instead of mutilated road installations that plague the inhabitants in the vicinity with noise and dust. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-11.ana.ud1232e1220	The first meeting of foreigners with Norway gives us many interesting and important reflections. Many of them are fascinating and impressive about the way we've been paving the way for the whole nation. There are ways up steep mountain sides and into the deepest fjords. Every year we will see the results of the enormous priority transport given by the red-green government and the majority here in the Storting. Norway is an important and central coastal nation. Our strength and position is not just due to the fact that we have a long-distance coast from nature. It is also due to the willingness and effort to expand infrastructure along the coast. Therefore, I am very pleased that the national transport plan adds up to an increase of 77 pst. in the territory of the Coastal Works. Compared to NTP 2006-2015, the annual budget of the Swedish Coastal Agency will be strengthened by 471 million. kr per year. I would characterise that as a significant investment in maritime transport. It consolidates and strengthens the role of Norway as a coastal nation. Maritime transport also plays a key role in achieving overall objectives of transport policy. Maritime transport is more climate friendly than road transport. And more transport by sea leads to fewer vehicles on the road – and thus shorter cows and fewer road accidents. In this Communication, the Government presents a package of measures to strengthen the position of maritime transport in the market. We are in favour of reducing the fees and charges for maritime transport. Modern and forward-looking port and water legislation has been presented by this government in the past. We have increased investments and maintenance of the maritime route and the organisation of combined transport solutions. The Government proposes to reduce the payment of users for maritime transport by abolishing the coastal fee and the annual port security fee. This will mean an annual fee and tax relief for maritime transport of 115 million. kr per year. It will improve the competitive conditions of maritime transport in relation to road transport. The need for a reduction in the user payment of maritime transport has been strongly signalled from the maritime transport industry for a long time, and it is a signal that the Government has taken seriously. Losing is an important measure to avoid ground-breaking and collisions. This is a service that is 100 pst. user-funded. I record that maritime transport actors believe that the obligation to provide services contributes to a high cost to industry. It is therefore very important, in the interests of industry and legitimacy of the financial instrument, that the service is as cost-effective as possible. Seaborne transport shall not be subject to unnecessary costs. A review of the pilot service is therefore being carried out with a view to improving efficiency. The Government shall come back with proposals that make the use of evidence of fairway more attractive compared to the use of pilots by placing a greater proportion of the total payment on the actual use of pilots. In addition, we will consider measures to facilitate the obtaining of fairway documents, including: seagoing requirements will be reduced in favour of actual competence testing. We also want to look at the extension of non-obligatory entrance corridors. Proposals for changes to the unloading service and the fees and charges to the Coast Guard will be sent to the hearing during the year. As regards new port and water laws, it was adopted by the Stortinget in February of this year and is due to enter into force on 1 January 2010. The new law facilitates good propulsion, safe movement and proper use and management of waters. The law facilitates efficient and commercial port operations with a focus on cost control. This in turn will benefit users and maritime transport. Among other things, port charges with general price fixing for the port services provided are replaced in order to ensure a level playing field between maritime transport and other modes of transport. It will also be easier to establish appropriate port cooperation in order to make better use of the infrastructure. NTP makes a strong commitment to fairway improvements, which will provide safer and more efficient shipping along the coast. The government has prioritised 42 major and minor measures in key traffic controllers, and proposes that almost DKK 3 billion be spent for this purpose over the next ten years. Fairway improvements are an important part of the process of making the seaway safe and efficient. Fewer accidents and shorter sailing times will make maritime transport even more environmentally friendly and attractive as a transport option. The Coast Guard Station is responsible for a significant number of navigational guidance devices in the fairways – lighthouses, brands and other things. These facilities are central to the maintenance of safe and efficient maritime traffic. In recent years, the Coast Guard has been focusing specifically on navigation facilities to meet the needs of fast boats for safe and efficient navigational guidance. There is a significant maintenance run on navigation installations and the Government therefore proposes to strengthen the annual allocation. The government also proposes to earmark funds for new vessels for the Coastal Works. These are vessels such as: used for the operation and maintenance of the navigation installations, and new vessels will contribute to more maintenance of the money. The coastal works renewal plan for the fleet provides for a transition to fewer and multifunctional construction and maintenance vessels and oil protection vessels. The five coastal works’ traffic centres are established along the coast in areas where traffic represents a special risk to maritime safety and the environment. Initial investment and reinvestment of technical equipment at the traffic centres are urgently needed. In order to maintain good monitoring of traffic along the coast, the Government will increase the allocation of investment in new equipment at the traffic centres by about 25 million. kr annually. The Government continues to give priority to efforts to improve fishing ports. It's set at NOK 2 billion for this purpose over the next ten years. It will facilitate forward-looking activity and ‘husband light’ along the whole coast. Nearly half of the fishing port funds are prioritised to actions in the north to build up under the Northern Territory Initiative of the Government. National transport plan 2010–2019 focuses in particular on the link between modes of transport and the development of intermodal hubs. It is defined, inter alia, by: a number of main network ports connected to the maritime and land-based transport networks under the responsibility of the State and the Government makes a commitment to improving road links to some of the parent ports. I have noticed that the Progress Party wants its own big message on maritime transport, and of course I share their commitment to strengthening this mode of transport. However, I believe that the interaction between different modes of transport is essential in order to strengthen maritime transport, and that it is therefore not appropriate to have a separate notification on maritime transport. All modes of transport should be treated in aggregate and with an equal planning horizon, as NTP has just stated. Essentially, I believe that NTP 2010–2019 deals with the issues that the Progress Party wishes to discuss in its own big business message. I am therefore very pleased that the Government's proposal for a national transport plan for the next ten years was launched a course that will strengthen maritime transport as a safe, environmentally friendly and competitive mode of transport and facilitate viable coastal communities. By addressing both charging and legal framework conditions, and by increasing the allocation of maritime infrastructure, the Government is launching a comprehensive package of instruments to strengthen maritime transport. Our policy is to enable carriers and shipowners to now assess the maritime transport option with new eyes. I would like to stress that the feedback from the maritime transport industry is that these are good measures. Finally, let me comment on one of the main dividing lines of Norwegian transport policy. It goes between the Progress Party and the Red-Green Government. The Progress Party is constantly distributing billions of dollars, and we will soon be up in several ‘fantasillions’ at the rate at which they distribute money. However, Norwegian roads do not receive asphalt from ‘fantasillions’. At least they do not get the bit of the policy of the Progress Party when they themselves are in power. In the last growing period, the Progress Party and the Bondevik government cooperated on three state budgets. They increased the transfer rate by 451 million. kr. The red-green government has used more for national transport than any government in the past. We've granted what we promised plus a little bit more. So in sum, we're going to spend $12.2 billion more on the road and the track than the previous forward party-supported Bondevik government. The government has launched several high-speed rail investigations in the period and has had a broad and good process. I am now recording that the Right is trying to take credit for this, but it is important to note that this work has the Stortinget, with the exception of the Progress Party, cross-politically supported about and been the drivers of, through our decisions on the need for more knowledge about the effects of such a transport option could provide. The conclusions of these investigations are not unambiguous, while investigations from other environments show that there is a market base for profitable high-speed rails in Norway. The majority of the Transport Committee has therefore emphasised in its opinion that further investigations must now be aimed at building and a decision-making basis must be available well in advance of the next roll-out of NTP. There must be no contrast between the deployment and upgrading of existing networks and the future deployment of high-speed rail systems, as some do. The railway we're building now, we're building for the next 100 years. We therefore need to know more about the transport needs and the possibilities a high-speed train could give us, not just for the next ten years, but far ahead. We have to set a competitive railway as a rail target for the next 100 years, so the speed will give itself. The question is not high-speed or not, but to combine speed requirements with our requirements for a double-track railway through the cities of the intercity sections. We get the answer at the next crossroads.

The purchase of new trains and the upgrading of existing materials must be subject to requirements. Both the Ministry and the NSB have a responsibility to make new and existing material available to disabled people. It is precisely when public contracts are to be concluded that concrete and obligatory requirements for better accessibility for people with disabilities can and must be made.

Poor man – that’s when you don’t get a loan and have no income or anything to play on in the future. Using future revenues and future returns to realize a project today is actually a well-known model to realise a higher standard of living than can be realised through cash payments today. That's actually what OPS is about. A lot of District Norway doesn't have time to sit and wait until we've saved the capital we need to realize the projects. We have to get them going today. What's behind it is that the return on getting the projects started is far higher than by sitting and waiting, and then actually OPS is the sensible solution to choose.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-11.ana.ud1232e2968	I want first of all to express the respect and admiration I feel for the Red Cross and all the others who work whole-heartedly to maintain safety and preparedness in the mountains and other places. They have members who give of themselves and leisure to say that anyone who drags into nature should know that if something goes wrong, skilled people will come on short notice to help them. In order for non-profit organisations to do this work in the best possible way and as quickly as possible, communication between the voluntary organisations and the regular emergency services is necessary. Such communication is largely done by means of a radio connection that communicates on a so-called closed network. In order to use a radio connection, a spectrum channel from the Postal and Telecommunications Authority is required. Spectrum is a restricted resource. Postal and telecommunications supervision manages and coordinates national and international spectrum management. In the creation of the Post and Telecommunications Authority, Stortinget decided that supervision should be self-financing, i.e. that the supervisory activities should be paid by those who use their supervision services. The fee regulation providing funding for the Post and Telecommunications Authority provides that the holder of spectrum rights for professional mobile radio – PMR – is to pay NOK 100000 for each network they have, and NOK 6250 for each 12.5 kHz channel that is disposed of. In Document No: The 8 proposal shows that the Red Cross pays an annual rate of charge for frequencies of about 10 %. 500,000 kroner. The organisations belonging to the Emergency Training Forum of Voluntary Organisations pay a total of approx. Two million. kr in the annual fee. The fee is part of the self-financing of the Post and Telecommunications Authority. The fees charged by supervision under the fee regulation are cost-oriented based on the average cost of the supervisory work on the various administrative areas. The basic principle is that all users should pay according to the actual costs of supervision in their field of work. Oversight includes: emphasis on the costs incurred in the matter in question and on the costs borne by each user responsible for the supervision, both directly and indirectly. If supervision were to exempt voluntary organisations from the obligation to pay fees, this would mean that other users would have to finance the activities of the voluntary organisations that might benefit from the exemption. It would be a solution that would clearly be contrary to the basic principle of cost-oriented self-financing on which supervision relies. There is currently no example of supervision granting such exemptions from the payment of a fee. I agree with those who have put forward the proposal that voluntary organisations make important contributions in a number of areas, including: in connection with rescue operations. It is clear that these organisations have a number of purchases of goods and services related to these activities, and there will therefore be questions about how they should be financed. It is therefore natural that a rather should consider looking at such funding schemes on a more superior and principled basis and look more closely at general support schemes towards voluntary organisations and on VAT exemptions. When it applies to the visit to the Central Party’s national meeting, it is absolutely firm. We're working to find good solutions for volunteering.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-11.ana.ud1232e3118	The 2008 credit notification provides a good presentation of the wide-ranging measures taken by the government in various countries to improve the situation in the financial market. There are also a number of measures taken in Norway. I'm not going to spend time commenting on all of these, but I'm just going to remind you that the development of the money market has at times been dramatic, and that it's been necessary for Norway Bank to go into extraordinary measures to ensure the functioning of the market. The situation is now in the process of normalising, although specific businesses report tightening credit and more expensive loans. The Norwegian economy is also the backbone of the recession, but less than most other countries. There are several reasons for that. While all demand towards industry falls in many countries, the petroleum sector in Norway is contributing to stabilising the economy. In addition, we have a relatively small industrial sector compared to countries such as the United Kingdom. Sweden and Germany, which are becoming much harder to cope with the international failure of demand. We also have a small financial sector, and Norwegian banks have little exposure to sub-prime loans – not least we have very sound government finances. As a consequence, we have followed the rule of action and not used the oil revenues without restraint, we now have the possibility to stimulate the economy even further when necessary. I would like to stress that the expansionary fiscal policy that is now being pursued is in line with the rule of action – not a violation of it, as some have claimed. Rather than conclude that the rule of action is irrelevant, as the Progress Party does, I agree with the Governor of the Central Bank, who, at the hearing of the Financial Committee on 14 May, stated that: ‘The rule of action as an anchor for fiscal policy in the longer term is immeasurably important.’.» It is little fruitful to make this a debate for or against market forces, or for or against regulation. There is no party – except perhaps the Progress Party – that thinks that the market is self-regulatory. However, there are differing views on how much and how financial markets should be regulated. The Right Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left seem to be most concerned that there must be a political framework that guarantees fair play rules and free competition. The government party believes that this is far from sufficient. The lot of progress shows in its note that: ‘The left side of Norwegian policy since the start of the financial crisis, in its propaganda, has proclaimed that the market economy is dead and that capitalism has failed’. This is a statement that I don't know about. Well-functioning markets in many cases lead to better resource use and greater well-being. However, it would often be desirable for different reasons to regulate the market. In the financial market area, regulation is important and necessary, including: due to systemic risk. If a large financial institution encounters problems, it will not only have consequences for the institution concerned, but for the financial system in the broadest sense. Breaking down the financial system, the real economy also becomes a framework. This risk has been clearly identified during the ongoing crisis. The recognition that financial markets in the world must be regulated — and in a better way than today — is quite different from proclaiming death over the market economy. At the heart of the Norwegian regulation of the financial market is the principle of a coherent and coherent regulation on capital adequacy. The Norwegian Financial Institution Code, unlike a number of other countries, is based on equal risks being treated equally. Therefore, banks, life insurance corporations and pension funds are subject to the same capital requirements regardless of the nature of the risk they take. Furthermore, our legislation makes it impossible for banks to circumvent capital requirements by securitisation lending. Another factor that can contribute to the reduction of the pooling of risks in the financial system is the non-recommendation of long-term risks. The party of progress claims that it is essentially the government’s regulations that have led to the financial crisis. Although the U.S. housing policy may have played a role, in my opinion it is an incorrect analysis. As I said, I think that the financial crisis has a number of complex reasons. However, I have no doubt that the lack of regulation of financial markets, not least in the United States, has contributed to a worsening of the crisis. According to the Credit Supervision, about half of the US financial market was unsupervised and capital requirements at the time of the crisis. For example, so-called securitisation — the issue of residential and immovable security securities — was: not regulated. In 2007 was 60 pst. of all US mortgages securitised. This contributed to uncertainty when it comes to where the financial system was at risk, leading to a crisis of confidence and a drought of capital between the banks. The party of progress seems to have a sometimes frightening belief that the market has a self-regulatory capacity. In the setting, it is shown that the market economy, unlike the planned economy, has the ability to correct errors and that it is just what happens when a experienced crack. Crisiss, according to the Progress Party, often clear up the economy and provide the basis for new growth. Politicians' task is to create a ground floor for optimism. I agree with the Progress Party that it is important that the governance facilitates innovation, and that the ability of the economy to convert is entirely decisive for how quickly we get out of the crisis. But I think it would be a serious mistake to follow the advice of the Progress Party and let the market settle even when the problems are as big as we now experience. The massive measures being put in place around the world show that no governmental powers – independent, in fact, of ideological and political standing – think this is the right recipe. Although Norway, compared with some other countries, has good and sound financial market regulation, there is every reason to look at how legislation can be made even better. I thought the governor made interesting contributions to this debate during the hearing with the committee. He first stated that the banks need more equity than they have today, so that they have ‘greater shock absorbers’, as he said, in bad times. This need is real, although the banks meet the existing statutory minimum capital adequacy requirements. Furthermore, he referred to an international discussion as to whether there should be countercyclical mechanisms embedded in the legislation. This could be an example. by increasing the capital requirement when the growth of the financial system is high. It will help to dampen the trend of cocyclical behaviour in the banking system. I share the opinion of the Governor of the Central Bank that before any such system is put in place, a thorough review of target formulation and responsibilities is important. I also agree that, in practice, we have a common Nordic financial market and that any changes must therefore be common to the whole Nordic region. I record that the Right Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left are concerned that large parts of our export-right industry mark the international crisis through a strong fall, both in prices and demand. I share this concern. Unfortunately, we have politicians restricted the ability to influence the international demand for Norwegian goods. However, the economic policy we pursue could have major consequences for the development of interest rates and exchange rates. The change in the price of the crown directly enters the bottom line of Norwegian export companies. We must therefore be very careful to pursue a policy that can lead to higher interest rates and strengthened crowns. This relationship implies that we must conduct a responsible economic policy even in difficult times as we now experience. I expect that we will come back to this issue in depth in the debate on the revised national budget, which we will have here in the Chamber next Friday.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-11.ana.ud1232e3313	The credit notification refers to the assumptions for financial stability in Norway, an assessment of the exercise of monetary policy in Norway in the last year and an activity interview by the central bank, which is required by law. The last year has shown that economic realities can turn rapidly and brutally. The whole world is now experiencing a sharp decline not only in economic growth but in gross domestic product. Norway, which has had a very high economic growth over the last few years, which has resulted in a strong economic upturn, is also aware of the impact of the real economic crisis on the body. A number of export industries are in a very demanding situation, currently being the woodworking industry. The credit risk of banks is largely linked to the development of the housing market. Until last year, Norwegian households had increased indebtedness, a longer average maturity of loans and a greater spread of non-debt loans, making borrowers more at risk from a sharp fall in housing prices. Last autumn, high interest rate increases were the challenge. Credit risk is now linked to the development of unemployment. The confidence crisis in the financial market is a result of longer-term policy choices, while the cause and effect may be difficult to understand. The crisis has been triggered by international deregulation originating in the United States. The US economist Jan Kregel has shown that new regulatory regimes have been exempt from banks ‘a lot of their old function as risk assessors and managed banks and brokerage houses to now specialize in putting together ‘packages’ of financial instruments and making their money to sell them, while not even performing their traditional role as risk assessors or risk managers themselves. These risky packages are not part of the asset side of the bank accounts after they have been sold». This is how the risk was borne by credulous buyers and society as a whole. The lifting of the Glass-Steagall Act in the United States in 1999, the law separating commercial banks and investment banks, together with the lifting of the equity limit of US investment banks in the spring of 2004 and the enormous growth in insurance against credit risk in the last few years, have contributed to the large scale of the initial financial crisis. Credit risk insurance proved to be highly co-cyclical. The problems of last year's harvest in the world's largest insurance company, AIG, were particularly related to a subsidiary that had sold credit risk insurance in large quantities. Without State intervention, any bankruptcy in the AIG would have a major impact on the world's capital markets. I would therefore like to stress how important it becomes for financial stability in the future that insurance companies keep to insure life and property rather than credit risk. At the same time, banks must strengthen their task of assessing risk. There has been a great deal of creativity in recent years about the creation of ever-new forms of financial and credit companies and financial products, where the authorities have been systematically in arrears in regulation. This has contributed to increasing risk and thus greater instability, and must in future be given new and reinforced attention by the regulatory authorities. This speaks for a far more proactive legislative role. Today's Business Today, Nordea-chef Gunn Wærsted says the following: ‘Some things will never be like before again.’ And on the other hand, ‘Dead low-cost loans. Adjourn, small banks with enormous ambitions. Adjourn, ‘gross’ bonus systems. At the same time, a number of new things are emerging, she thinks: Good old-fashioned banking. Strengthen supervision and control. Increased internationalisation.’ Yes, these are important headlines in the work of the new financial crisis commission that the Government is about to appoint. In today's business, Gunn Wærsted also asks if she should point her finger at someone after the crisis, who it is to. Her answer is: ‘It does not reach with one finger. I need my whole hand. Everyone needs to be self-contained: banks, authorities, credit rating agencies, but also customers.’ That is, in my opinion, a wise answer. We are facing a systemic crisis both internationally and nationally. Internationally, the United Nations Commission led by Joseph Stiglitz has provided its investigation to the Secretary-General of the United Nations. This will be addressed at the UN early in the autumn and high expectations will be placed on the results of that work. The government’s financial crisis commission must do a thorough job based on national circumstances and needs, but with a strong emphasis on analysing the nature of the international crisis. This will facilitate major system changes that I hope will come, and therefore we will have a more stable credit and financial system in Norway as well.

After nine hours of wanting and dreaming about how roads and tracks should be laid in this country, there may not be any basis for spending too much time on such big questions as what we have up now in this evening. I think that sometimes it can be that the most important things should not be the victims of the greatest debates and games in this room. But I still want to say a little bit about this which seems to be a little ideological debate between the extreme left and the extreme right, about who is best in dealing with the market economy. I would say, as a centre politician, that the answer is neither on the right side nor on the left, but there are balanced solutions that lay a good political framework around a market economy. Should you ask yourself questions about how to eliminate the market economy, if someone had to have such desires and dreams, I think it's pretty easy to cut out all the regulation. A non-regulatory market economy will only destroy itself. That way, the actors will avoid competition by buying each other up, and in that way we will lose the market. So any market economy will require sensible and good regulation, and as much regulation as is necessary, and that makes the market work. I believe that in the situation we are in now, when we are in the midst of a financial crisis and try to have some assessments of what we are going to do further, it will be about how we are going to regulate – perhaps more, but at least better, to ensure that the market economy, not least in the financial field, will work better in the future. We're processing the credit notification. I think it might be so interesting to deal with the credit notification in one, two or three years, where we'll learn more clearly what we've learned from this crisis. Now we're in the middle of it, and it's pretty bland from many people about where the road goes, other than that there's a road that's not the way back. I'm going to contribute some strings in that context. I think we are going to have a completely different debate on monetary policy in Norway from what we might have had so far, precisely because of the need to find the reasonable balance between promoting price stability, which is an important part of the mandate of the Bank of Norway today, and promoting financial stability. One can see situations where this can, in effect, stand against each other and to some extent be conflicting interests. I think it will be important in the future in the debate on monetary policy. There seems to be a broad consensus, in an international context, on how we use the IMF and strengthen the IMF. It's going to be pretty central, and I think it'll be broad, cross-political consensus in Norway to contribute to it. We need more global policies in a situation where the global economy turns out to need clearer frameworks. The last area that I think we're going to work a lot on the next few years, as a follow-up to this, is the question of what we're going to do differently in regulation. I have addressed this in the other debates we have had in the middle of crisis management, that I think we need to look at some things here. The left says something about it in notes, both with others and alone. The most important thing is to be able to contribute to countercyclical capital requirements, which I think are going to become relevant, and which the committee itself learned a little about when we were on a study trip to Spain, which has just had it, and which seems to have managed to take its banking business surprisingly well through the crisis so far, despite the fact that the country is in a dramatic crisis – and of course the issue of regulating, through law and by other means, equity and more equity access to the banks. We need more and better regulation. We've pointed out some directions from the opposition. I also hope that the Government in its further work will address these challenges.

But let me just make a few brief comments, because I share the representative Sponheim's view that the 2008 credit report is historical. But it is enough for the next year and the year after that that you will have so much evidence on the table that you can become more bombastic in the conclusion of what is the right, if any, new governance. Just a few comments on the debate first: no one, as I know, has believed that the market economy as such would be abolished after this financial crisis. I fully agree with the chairman of the committee, Mr Sandal, in that perspective. But at the same time, it can be said with a broad pen that the market fundamentalists, those who have believed that markets regulate themselves, those who have in a way resorted to the ideological direction, have been wrong. Not least, we see this in the debate in various international forums, where we are now looking to coordinate, co-operate and find global and international rules that can be involved in precisely giving a counterbalance to an increasingly globalised world economy. That a crisis that starts in the United States can have so great consequences in the rest of the world, in Europe, in our neighbouring countries, says something about the need for close international cooperation in this area. So to an observation on the fixed rate, where I see that both one and the other have points of view. One is the governor, and the other is Heikki Holmås. I don't think there's any reason why some members of the finance committee are going to flirt with it, because it's really quite remarkable how many borrowers in Norway have floating interest rates. Circa. 5 pst. of the mortgage borrowers in Norway have fixed interest rates. In Sweden it is 60 pst., and in Denmark it is 45 pst. It has the function that the reductions in interest rates of the Bank of Norway are increasing faster and more directly in relation to household demand. As we see it, we have a more effective monetary policy in such a situation as it is now. But that also means that the representative Holmås stressed, namely that the way we finance housing is helping to build up under the significant fluctuations that are in the housing market in terms of housing prices, and that makes people more vulnerable to significant fluctuations in interest rates. But that's part of the discussion about this. So some of the things that have been central during this year. After all, we have implemented comprehensive measures in the banking sector, first in terms of liquidity. I guess a lot of people didn't realize how close you were to a collapse in the financial systems this fall. Norway’s Bank, the Ministry of Finance and the Stortinget were quickly in place with measures that allowed us to move in these markets again. They're not quite back to normal, either in terms of impact or otherwise. Through this exchange mechanism, where Stortinget gave an authorisation to use NOK 350 billion, it is now auctioned for NOK 130 billion. It can be said that those measures have helped to ensure that liquidity problems are mitigated and avoided. Since then, we have made further efforts, including: by the State Financial Fund. It is at the earliest made to have a lot of information about how the State Financial Fund and the State Bond Fund have actually worked. However, it is quite obvious that the Financial Committee will be provided with all the information they want – even before the next credit notification, if necessary. It is also obvious that if we find weaknesses or new challenges in this area, we are ready to get back to the Storing with it. But I might call out a little warning because it's more expensive for business to finance now. I hear many examples of business leaders saying they lack funding for projects. On the one hand it is possible to obtain funding, but they think it is too expensive, on the other hand there may be projects that are poor now, but that were also poor two years ago. So the reality here might be a little bit more complex. Much of the discussion going on internationally is really about getting international financial regulation standards that are very similar to what we have at national level. What is one of the themes is, for example: single supervision, not fragmentation or sector-specific supervision, but single supervision for the whole financial sector. That is what we have in Norway, which means that we have effective supervision of our financial sector, and that is that all the risks should be regulated in principle equally across the board. That means there's no place to hide risks in such a way, as we've seen has been the cause of the financial crisis in many other places. This means, for example: Norwegian pension funds, life insurance corporations, etc. are subject to capital adequacy requirements on capital management risks. Credit institutions issuing covered bonds are subject to capital adequacy requirements in line with banks. It's the kind of example that's good regulation. This has clearly contributed to the strength of Norwegian financial institutions. It is also the case that the group law we have for financial groups contributes to the overall capital requirements for financial groups, and it discourages the fact that any problems in one group company also easily lead to problems for other group companies. Nor is it a matter of course in the legislation of other countries. There is a clear need for significant international cooperation in this context, and I agree with those who have raised the issue of whether the regulation vis-à-vis the banks is co-cyclical, and the desire to look at regulation that makes it more countercyclical. There are also themes in international work that are currently underway, and are of the things we can learn from following this financial crisis. What also has to be part of learning are bonus systems and pay systems, which in themselves have worked in the way that we have had so-called leadership incentives, which have led many leaders to take very high risks with very short-term horizons. The discussion on how to achieve pay systems that are not in themselves involved in destabilising or encouraging the taking of that type of risk is a very important and important debate. It's not just about fair distribution, it's about the level of pay you have. It is simply a matter of having very unhealthy incentives in a financial sector. In Norway, the financial crisis has been much less dramatic than in many other countries. In part, this is due to the regulations that I have been subject to. On the one hand, it is because the lessons learned from the banking crisis we had in the early 1990s are in the hands of the regulatory authorities. It also sits in both walls and heads of many of the bankers themselves, and agrees to explain that so far we have not experienced the same drama that many other countries have done.
